14:I[8669,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DateSidebar"]
15:I[44692,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DailyView"]
16:T470,The experimental design scales down Common Crawl and its filtered variants while preserving their relative sizes, then varies model size and training steps over these pools. Increasing steps induces data repetition; increasing model size raises compute per step. This setup directly probes the tradeoff introduced by filtering: it improves average document quality but reduces the available token pool. The authors compare the best performance achievable under different compute budgets rather than relying only on fixed-token or fixed-step comparisons. They then fit empirical relationships among pool size, number of training steps, and model size to predict when no filtering should become optimal. A second set of experiments injects deliberately degraded data, including random strings and documents with shuffled word order, to test whether large models merely tolerate noise or can still extract weak signals from it. A post-hoc theoretical model explains the observed pattern: cleaning helps imperfect, capacity-limited learners, while sufficiently capable learners can benefit from modeling the noisy generation process itself.17:T51d,The key scale numbers are informative. A heavily filtered dataset such as DCLM-Baseline keeps roughly 1% of the original Common Crawl, yielding about 3.8T tokens, while the DCLM-Pool scale is around 240T tokens. The study spans about two orders of magnitude in compute, and the same qualitative trend persists as the pool scale increases: once the model is large enough and trained long enough, the full pool overtakes filtered pools. The fitted scaling law suggests that the 240T-token Common Crawl pool may become optimal around 1e30 FLOPs. Filtering still wins in lower-compute regimes, which explains why earlier filtering studies saw strong benefits. The noisy-data experiments add a useful boundary case. Random strings hurt at low compute, but sufficiently trained large models narrow the gap. Shuffled-word documents are more revealing: they destroy syntax and local semantics while preserving unigram statistics, yet large models can still benefit from them. This indicates that useful signal is not limited to human-defined high-quality prose; token frequencies, entity coverage, topical diversity, and long-tail lexical statistics also matter. The authors also state an important limitation: adversarial, non-factual, or safety-critical data that resembles high-quality text could still be harmful.18:T6a5,The paper fixes a standard LLaMA-3 Transformer backbone (explicitly avoiding typical byte-LM downsampling modules like strided convs or local encoders) and trains on UTF-8 bytes using TorchTitan, with FineWeb-Edu as data. To obtain controllable subword information without switching the modeling unit, the same text is also tokenized with the LLaMA-3 BPE tokenizer to extract subword boundaries/segments aligned to byte positions. Evaluation is unified via bits-per-byte cross-entropy on a held-out FineWeb-Edu validation split.

On top of the byte baseline, the authors introduce a series of low-overhead interventions, each designed to mimic one hypothesized benefit of subword tokenization while minimizing confounders:
- Throughput/sequence compression: simulate the advantage of shorter sequences (more raw bytes seen per unit compute) within the byte pipeline.
- Vocabulary/output-layer scaling: simulate the capacity effects associated with larger discrete vocabularies, focusing on embedding/output-layer parameterization.
- Boundary priors: inject BPE boundary signals as explicit structure so the model can exploit segmentation-like cues without changing the base unit.
- Look-ahead/task easiness: approximate the “future hint” implicit in subword segmentation decisions that require looking ahead.
- Multi-byte prediction: add multi-token (multi-byte) prediction heads to emulate the fact that one subword prediction corresponds to predicting a byte n-gram.

The design choice is deliberate: keep the backbone and most hyperparameters fixed, and localize changes to input annotations, output heads, and objective granularity so attribution stays focused on tokenization-related factors.19:T511,On the model side, Gemini 3.5 Flash optimizes inference efficiency and agent/coding capabilities based on the Gemini 3 multimodal foundation, maintaining multimodal understanding while reducing latency to the level of the Flash series. Gemini Omni integrates Gemini's general knowledge base with Google's generative media models' physical modeling capabilities, supports unified generation from any input to any output, launches with video generation support, and includes built-in imperceptible SynthID watermarking. On the platform side, the agent-first Google Antigravity development platform connects the full pipeline from Gemini API to AI Studio and Android Studio. On the search side, it upgrades to a multimodal intelligent search box, supports background-running information agents, and integrates Antigravity's generative UI and mini-app generation capabilities. Unlike existing solutions, Gemini 3.5 Flash is the first fast model that outperforms Gemini 3.1 Pro on coding and agent benchmarks, breaking the common perception that fast models have weaker capabilities. Gemini Omni achieves end-to-end integration of general knowledge and generative media capabilities, instead of the common approach of attaching independent generation modules to understanding-focused multimodal models.1a:T55d,TokenDrift keeps the DDLM architecture and sampler fixed and adds a drifting-style refinement loss.

1) Differentiable lift from categorical outputs to continuous features: instead of sampling hard tokens, it computes expected token embeddings from the model’s per-position categorical distributions (soft-token embeddings). These are fed into a frozen semantic encoder to obtain continuous features.

2) Drifting in a frozen semantic space: in that feature space, it estimates an attraction–repulsion drift field V(y; P_data, P_theta), designed to be anti-symmetric so the signal vanishes at equilibrium (when model and data match). This preserves the fixed-point property that motivates drifting.

3) Stop-gradient drift target: it forms a drifted feature target y* (stop-gradient) and trains the DDLM to match y* (direct feature-space matching). The paper also explores alternative couplings—e.g., converting drift into a logit-space “mirror teacher”, and mixing with the original denoising loss—and reports that direct feature-space drifting yields the most stable balance between sample quality and entropy.

4) Why soft-token matters: replacing the soft lift with a hard straight-through surrogate significantly degrades generation, supporting the claim that preserving predictive uncertainty in the semantic space is crucial for effective drift learning.1b:T456,The authors build a high-quality 10T-token corpus with fine-grained domain separation and pretrain MoE models of varying sizes from scratch. The MoE setup replaces standard FFNs with multiple experts and uses a router to assign tokens to experts, which also enables analysis of expert specialization. The core experimental design has two controlled ablations. First, they isolate pure code, defined strictly as executable functions or program fragments, excluding explanations, comments, and instructional text. This separates programming data from cross-domain reasoning traces. Second, they ablate the mathematics portion of the corpus to measure interactions between math and code. They then introduce a more targeted class of structured, mathematically grounded reasoning samples. By increasing this subset while keeping the total math-token budget fixed, they test whether explicit multi-step structure is more useful than simply adding more math tokens. Expert-routing distributions and hook-based analyses are used to inspect whether the new data changes expert allocation in a stable or disruptive way.1c:T937,技术路线由架构与目标两部分组成，而且两者是联动设计，不是简单相加。架构上，HRM-Text 采用双时间尺度递归：慢速 H 模块负责较稳定的高层语义状态，快速 L 模块负责局部迭代细化。一次前向包含两个 high-level cycle，每个 cycle 内执行三次 L 更新再执行一次 H 更新，最终用最后的 H 状态输出 token logits。这样做的意图是用重复计算换参数规模，让模型在较少参数和较少数据下仍有较深的推理/整合过程。问题在于，重复应用同一变换会把 Transformer 中 norm placement 的老问题放大。为此作者引入 MagicNorm 和 warmup deep credit assignment。根据正文描述，MagicNorm 试图兼顾 PreNorm 与 PostNorm 的优点：既约束前向激活方差，避免递归堆叠时状态发散，又尽量保留稳定的梯度通路，减少深递归下的优化困难。配套上使用 parameterless RMSNorm、SwiGLU、RoPE 和 sigmoid-gated self-attention，说明作者在尽量压缩不必要自由度，优先保训练稳定。warmup deep credit assignment 则是一个很关键但容易被忽略的训练技巧：训练初期只对最后 2 个 recurrent step 回传梯度，随后再扩展到最后 5 步，等于显式控制有效反向深度，先学短程信用分配，再逐步放开长程依赖。这和 curriculum on optimization depth 很像，是递归 LM 能否训起来的关键之一。

目标函数上，作者没有走标准 raw-text next-token，而是直接从 instruction-response pairs 做 task-completion pretraining，只在 response token 上计算 NLL，即优化 -log P(x_a | x_q)。同时配 PrefixLM mask，让 instruction 部分双向可见、response 部分保持因果生成。这里的设计选择很鲜明：既然模型最终大多用于条件生成，就不再把大量算力花在预测 prompt-like 文本、网页噪声或任务无关续写上，而是把训练信号集中到“给定条件，完成回答”这件事。和纯 encoder-decoder 不同，PrefixLM 仍保留单流生成接口；和标准 causal LM 不同，它允许 instruction 侧更充分的信息整合。论文真正有意思的地方在于，它不是只换了一个 mask，而是把数据形态、损失定义和架构归纳偏置一起改了，形成一个面向 sample efficiency 的整体 recipe。1d:Ta1f,The method is a coupled redesign of architecture and objective rather than an isolated component swap. On the architecture side, HRM-Text uses dual-timescale recurrence: a slow H module maintains a relatively stable high-level semantic state, while a fast L module performs local iterative refinement. One forward pass consists of two high-level cycles; each cycle runs three L updates followed by one H update, and token logits are produced from the final H state. The intended trade-off is clear: use repeated computation instead of proportionally increasing parameter count, so the model can perform deeper integration under smaller budgets.

The hard part is optimization. Repeatedly applying the same transformation amplifies the classic normalization-placement problem already familiar from Transformers. The paper introduces MagicNorm and warmup deep credit assignment to make this viable. From the full text, MagicNorm is designed to preserve bounded forward activation variance while keeping backward optimization stable, effectively trying to capture the good parts of both PreNorm and PostNorm under recurrence. The surrounding design choices—parameterless RMSNorm, SwiGLU, RoPE, and sigmoid-gated self-attention—suggest a deliberate bias toward stability and reduced unnecessary flexibility. Warmup deep credit assignment is especially important: early in training, gradients are backpropagated only through the final two recurrent steps, and later expanded to the final five. This explicitly controls effective backward depth, allowing the model to first learn short-range credit assignment before exposing it to longer recurrent dependencies.

On the objective side, the paper abandons standard raw-text next-token pretraining. HRM-Text is trained from scratch on instruction-response pairs, with NLL computed only on the response tokens, i.e. optimizing -log P(x_a | x_q). This is paired with a PrefixLM mask: full bidirectional attention over the instruction prefix, causal generation over the response. The motivation is sharp. If the model is mostly used for conditional generation at inference time, then spending most of the budget predicting prompt-like text, web boilerplate, or task-irrelevant continuations may be wasteful. Compared with encoder-decoder training, PrefixLM preserves a single-stream generative interface; compared with standard causal LM, it allows richer integration over the conditioning context. The interesting part is that the paper changes data form, loss support, and architectural prior together into one sample-efficiency-oriented recipe.1e:T6c0,The headline result is the budget comparison. The authors claim that HRM-Text, trained from scratch on only 40B unique tokens, reaches the performance neighborhood of contemporary open models such as Llama, Qwen, Gemma, OLMo, Ouro, and Huginn on most benchmarks, while using roughly 100–900× fewer training tokens and 96–432× less estimated training compute. If those numbers are supported under fair accounting, this is not a routine gain in data efficiency; it materially changes what is feasible in the low-budget regime. The wording matters, though: the claim is about entering the same performance band on many benchmarks, not uniformly surpassing those baselines.

Three experimental blocks deserve close attention. First, stability and training dynamics: ablations for MagicNorm and warmup deep credit assignment should reveal whether training diverges, becomes noisy, or converges to much worse solutions without them. Loss curves, activation/gradient statistics, and sensitivity to backprop depth are central here. Second, objective ablations: instruction-response-only training, response-only loss, and PrefixLM masking versus standard causal LM or raw-text training. This determines whether the gains come mainly from the architecture or from a more focused data/objective design. Third, compute and data efficiency comparisons against open baselines under roughly matched parameter scales or training budgets. The paper’s main figure and token/compute table should be read carefully: unique tokens versus seen tokens, measured versus estimated compute, and whether the benchmark suite is especially favorable to instruction-conditioned models. That distinction matters when interpreting “beyond scaling.”1f:T717,This one deserves a full read. Start with the introduction and isolate the three assumptions being challenged: Transformer as the only path, raw-text AR as the only path, and extreme budget as a necessity. Then move directly to the HRM forward schedule and make sure the H/L update pattern is fully clear; otherwise the stabilization section will feel opaque. The next priority is the stabilization machinery—MagicNorm and warmup deep credit assignment. That is likely the most underappreciated part of the paper and the part most worth reproducing, because many recurrent language-model ideas fail not conceptually but optimization-wise. Read the normalization equations, residual pathways, backward-depth schedule, and the corresponding ablations together.

The second major section to study is the objective design: why compute loss only on the response, why use PrefixLM instead of a standard causal mask, and how the paper argues that much of raw-text prediction is inefficient for its target use case. Read this skeptically. The key question is whether the recipe genuinely improves general sample efficiency or simply aligns better with instruction-heavy evaluations.

In the experiments, prioritize the main result table, the stability ablations, and the token/compute comparison against open models. If time is limited, at least read Figure 1, Table 4, the stabilization ablation figures, and the objective ablation table. Expect 60–90 minutes for a reliable judgment. Three follow-up questions are especially worth digging into: whether dual-timescale recurrence opens a scalable test-time compute axis, whether response-only pretraining weakens broad world-modeling coverage, and how much of the result is actually driven by the composition and quality control of the 40B instruction-response corpus.20:T456,DynaTrain’s key idea is the Virtual Parameter Space (VPS): represent parameters/gradients/optimizer states as coordinates in an unsharded global logical space, with partition metadata. Any concrete parallelism layout becomes a deterministic projection from VPS to each rank’s local sub-region. Switching layouts is then reduced to computing geometric intersections between source and destination sub-VPS regions, avoiding a combinatorial set of bespoke conversion rules.
- State Routing Planner: computes per-rank send/recv/retain sets purely in VPS coordinates.
- State Transition Engine: lowers the plan into an optimized, memory-aware execution pipeline—packing fragments into contiguous buffers, promoting P2P to collectives when possible, and enforcing a provably deadlock-free schedule.
- Elastic Device Manager: decouples logical strategies from physical process groups and overlaps new-world communicator initialization with ongoing training to hide topology-reconfiguration latency.
Implemented as a middleware layer between PyTorch and frameworks like Megatron-LM, aiming for minimal intrusion.21:T4a7,The authors start from a pretrained dense video generation model and convert its FFN blocks into MoE modules. Routed experts are cloned one-to-one from the original dense FFN weights. Shared experts are zero-initialized for verification, to preserve output equivalence, and initialized with tiny non-zero noise such as 1e-4 for training. Routers are randomly initialized and trained from scratch. This controlled setup is designed to expose routing failures rather than expert-capacity differences. The paper compares an architectural progression from linear routers to cross-attention routers and defines several failure modes. Global deadlock refers to broad collapse into a small number of experts. Selective deadlock appears after global collapse is reduced, where a subset of layers still degenerates into near single-expert usage. The reported U-shaped deadlock distribution means deadlocked layers concentrate in shallow visual-processing layers and deep semantic-integration layers, while middle layers remain more balanced. The functional redundancy hypothesis interprets some deadlock as phased resource allocation under visual-token redundancy, rather than pure optimizer failure.22:T424,The proposed method, BA-Att, evaluates block quality directly in a downsampled block space before selecting sparse attention blocks. This differs from methods that sample fixed regions in the high-resolution attention map and then aggregate them. The paper defines an oracle attention map induced by post-downsampling computation and studies the approximation gap between the pre-downsampled scheme and this oracle. The analysis shows that large intra-block norm ranges in QK pairs make attention more dispersed and degrade block approximation. BA-Att therefore adds a lightweight norm-sorting module with O(LD) complexity to cluster high-activation tokens. It also adds a covariance-compensated correction, approximating the full covariance term with diagonal combinations of QK variances, reducing the correction cost from O(LD^2) to O(LD). Compared with learned gating methods, it requires no extra training. Compared with fixed sparse patterns such as tail, vertical-slash, or anti-diagonal heuristics, it searches over the downsampled space more directly.23:T871,TORQ stands for Two-Level Orthogonal Rotation. The method decomposes MXFP4 activation quantization error into two nearly orthogonal subproblems and addresses each with a dedicated orthogonal transform.

The first component is macro-equilibrium rotation, which targets inter-block variance imbalance. Using a construction motivated by the Schur-Horn theorem, the method applies an inter-block orthogonal transform to redistribute activation energy more evenly across blocks. The intuition is simple: if block variances are more balanced, the shared exponent is less likely to be dominated by a few extreme blocks, so fewer small values are rounded to zero. The paper further argues, under its error model, that variance equalization is optimal or near-optimal for minimizing global quantization error. This is a meaningful departure from prior rotation-based PTQ methods such as FlatQuant or OSTQuant, which were largely designed for uniform integer grids and focused on suppressing coordinate-wise outliers.

The second component is micro-alignment rotation, which targets intra-block codebook utilization imbalance. After the macro-level balancing, TORQ performs another orthogonal rotation within groups or blocks so that local values spread more evenly across the MXFP4 quantization intervals. The paper frames this through a maximum-entropy principle: under a fixed energy budget, a distribution that occupies codebook bins more evenly uses the available representational capacity more effectively. In other words, the macro stage decides how energy is distributed across blocks, while the micro stage decides how the codebook is used within each block.

Implementation-wise, TORQ is a retraining-free PTQ method. The rotation matrices are constructed in closed form or near closed form from a small calibration set, without gradient-based retraining. At inference time, the rotations can be fused around linear layers with minimal parameter and compute overhead. A key point throughout the paper is that TORQ is not a direct transfer of integer-oriented rotation methods to MXFP4; its objective is explicitly tailored to the block floating-point structure.24:T5b3,From the provided full-text excerpt, the experiments cover mainstream open LLM families including LLaMA and Qwen, and compare against methods such as SingleQuant, DARTQuant, FlatQuant, and OSTQuant. The main empirical claim is that TORQ preserves model quality more reliably under MXFP4 activation quantization, especially in layers where heavy-tailed activations make shared-exponent quantization particularly fragile.

The most informative results are likely not just end-task scores, but the intermediate diagnostics that support the paper’s mechanism:
1. whether block variance histograms become flatter after the macro rotation;
2. whether the fraction of small-magnitude values quantized to zero drops materially;
3. whether MXFP4 codebook occupancy becomes more uniform and codebook collapse is alleviated;
4. how much gain comes from macro-only, micro-only, and the full two-level method.

The introduction already gives two important quantitative observations that motivate the method: more than 90% of small-magnitude elements in a block can be zeroed out when the shared exponent is driven up, and roughly 50% of the codeword range is activated by only 10% of the data during real inference. Those numbers are central to the paper’s argument. When reading the full paper, it is worth checking the exact perplexity or downstream accuracy gaps in the main tables, as well as robustness across block sizes, calibration-set sizes, and model scales.25:T543,Read this one by following the error model first, not the final benchmark table.

Start with the introduction and the motivation figures. The paper stands or falls on whether the two claimed mismatches are real and consequential: inter-block variance imbalance and intra-block codebook collapse.

Then go to the theory section around the Schur-Horn-based construction. The key question is not every proof detail, but why block-energy equalization can be cast as an orthogonal rotation problem, and what assumptions are needed for the optimality claim. Those assumptions define the method’s real scope.

Next, inspect the micro-alignment section carefully. Check whether the maximum-entropy argument is a strict optimization target or mainly a design heuristic, and how it translates into an actual rotation matrix. If this part is approximate, the practical gains may come mostly from the macro stage.

In the experiments, prioritize three kinds of figures: block variance histograms, codebook occupancy or zeroing-ratio plots, and the main quality tables. That sequence makes it easier to connect the proposed mechanism to the final performance.

A quick pass takes about 25–35 minutes. A serious read, especially if the goal is to adapt the ideas to quantization-aware training or quantization-friendly pretraining, will take around an hour.26:T905,The paper has two main technical components.

The first is a unified theoretical comparison of EDEN, RabitQ, and TurboQuant. The authors place them in a common randomized quantization framework and compare them under three criteria: reconstruction MSE, expected inner-product distortion, and high-probability bit-complexity guarantees. The conclusion is deliberately nuanced: no method dominates across all criteria. EDEN and TurboQuant are favorable for MSE, EDEN is also strong for expected inner-product distortion, while RabitQ offers the strongest high-probability control. A particularly useful aspect of the analysis is that it disentangles the contribution of the codebook from that of the dequantization rule. Many empirical comparisons blur these two factors, which makes the conclusions hard to interpret.

The second component is Block-Sphere Quantization, or BlockQuant. Like prior rotation-based quantizers, it starts with a random rotation so that the vector becomes closer to isotropic. The key difference is that EDEN and TurboQuant quantize coordinate-wise using one-dimensional marginals, whereas BlockQuant groups coordinates into small blocks and uses the exact block marginal distribution induced by the unit sphere to define centroids. In spirit, this resembles product quantization because it works blockwise, but there is a crucial distinction: the codebook is not learned from data. Instead, it is derived analytically from the known spherical distribution after random rotation. That makes the method easier to analyze and removes the need for codebook training.

The paper also provides lower-bound analysis. It revisits the Shannon lower bound for this setting, corrects it for the problem at hand, and argues that as the block size increases, BlockQuant approaches the ideal spherical quantization limit. This is an important point: the move from coordinate-wise quantization to small-block quantization is not presented as a heuristic tweak, but as a geometrically principled step toward the right asymptotic object.

On the implementation side, BlockQuant requires nearest-centroid search within each block. The paper claims that efficient approximate search keeps runtime comparable to prior methods, so the proposal is intended to be practical rather than purely theoretical.27:T5a4,The experiments appear to operate at three levels.

First, the paper evaluates distortion metrics directly. On real embedding datasets, BlockQuant is reported to achieve lower reconstruction error and lower inner-product distortion than EDEN, RabitQ, and TurboQuant. That matches the expectation-based theory.

Second, the paper likely studies how performance changes with bit-width and block size, and whether larger blocks move the method closer to the ideal spherical quantization bound. This part matters because it tests whether the gains are only asymptotic or already visible in the low-bit regime that matters in practice.

Third, the paper includes an LLM-facing application: KV-cache quantization for long-context inference. The claim is that BlockQuant improves cache quantization quality while keeping runtime in the same ballpark through approximate nearest-centroid search. The provided excerpt does not include exact numbers, so the right way to read the paper is to verify the concrete setup: which models are used, what cache bit-width is tested, how perplexity or long-context task quality changes, and whether the gains over prior rotation-based quantizers are stable.

If there are ablations, the most informative ones are likely block size selection, the accuracy-speed tradeoff from approximate search, sensitivity to the random rotation, and robustness when the vector distribution deviates from the spherical assumption.28:T4ad,The reading order matters here.

Start with the unified comparison section, especially the summary table and the formal definitions of the three criteria: MSE, expected inner-product distortion, and high-probability bit complexity. The paper is valuable precisely because it refuses to collapse these into a single notion of “better.”

Then read the BlockQuant construction. The main goal is not to memorize formulas, but to understand why blockwise spherical marginals are a better fit than coordinate-wise marginals after random rotation. It helps to compare this mentally with product quantization, lattice-style quantization, and TurboQuant’s coordinate-wise design.

Next, read the lower-bound section and the analysis of block size. That part tells you whether the method has a principled advantage or only an empirical one.

In the experiments, prioritize the distortion curves and the KV-cache application tables. For an LLM researcher, the key question is whether the cache-compression gains are stable enough to justify the extra nearest-centroid search.

A quick read takes around 30 minutes. A careful read of the theory and lower-bound arguments will likely take 1 to 1.5 hours.29:T7c9,The method, called Graft, is a training-free and lossless hybrid tree construction framework built around a prune-then-graft strategy.

Step one is pruning. Similar to prior dynamic-tree methods, Graft uses calibrated confidence checkpoints to decide when low-confidence branches should be cut early. The important shift is interpretive: the removed branches are not treated merely as deleted candidates, but as released candidate budget. The paper argues that pruning-only methods are fundamentally limited because they must remain subtrees of the original static tree. As a result, any pruning error directly lowers coverage and therefore lowers the achievable accepted length.

Step two is grafting. Instead of leaving the freed slots empty, or spending them on deeper uncertain autoregressive drafting, Graft fills them with retrieval-based continuations. The total verification budget remains identical to that of the original static tree, but the candidate pool now becomes a hybrid of a pruned parametric subtree and retrieval branches. This matters because retrieval branches are not constrained to be descendants of the retained subtree, so they can recover some of the valid continuations lost to pruning and thereby move beyond the usual latency-MAT tradeoff.

Implementation is designed to be GPU-friendly. Retrieval branches are rooted at the current token and prepared in parallel with autoregressive drafting. A GPU-resident adjacency matrix stores local transition information, is initialized during warm-up, and updated online using target-model verification signals. This avoids CPU-side lookup and synchronization, preserves the standard tree-attention verification path, and does not require extra target forward passes. The paper also argues that the design becomes more attractive with long contexts: autoregressive drafting gets more expensive, making pruning more valuable, while longer contexts provide richer local transition patterns that improve retrieval hit rate.2a:T54f,The provided full text is limited to the introduction and motivation, so the exact experimental numbers are not available here. Still, the evaluation structure is clear from the paper’s setup. First, Graft is positioned against dense static-tree baselines such as EAGLE-3 and against pruning-only dynamic-tree methods such as DDD, SVIP, ECHO, and related variants. The key metrics are expected to be mean accepted tokens, end-to-end latency, and realized speedup rather than acceptance rate alone. Second, the paper explicitly states that the final verification budget is kept identical to the original static tree, which is important because it isolates the gain as a consequence of budget reallocation rather than simply adding more candidates. Third, the authors claim stronger benefits under long-context generation, so the experiments likely vary prompt or output length and analyze how retrieval hit rate changes with context length.

The most informative ablations, if present in the main text, would be: the effect of calibrated pruning checkpoints, the number and placement of grafted retrieval branches, the necessity of warm-up and online updates for the GPU adjacency matrix, and comparisons among retrieval-only, pruning-only, and hybrid construction. Those would explain why the method works more clearly than a single aggregate speedup table.2b:T7a7,The method has two main components. First is MOCD, Modality-specific Outlier Channel Decoupling. Using calibration activations, the authors identify that vision tokens tend to exhibit long-tailed channel distributions with a few very large-magnitude channels, while text tokens show a different outlier pattern that is still identifiable channel-wise. Rather than learning one shared transformation over all channels, they partition input channels into three disjoint groups: modality-compatible main channels, text-specific outlier channels, and vision-specific outlier channels. The partition is not based on a single heuristic. Vision-side channels are identified through magnitude saliency, while text-side channels are selected using response consistency, aiming to capture channels that are especially sensitive for one modality and should not share the same quantization path with the other. After partitioning, both activations and weights are split accordingly and quantized through separate branches. The key idea is not to add model capacity, but to prevent one modality’s outliers from dictating the quantization scale of the other.

Second is ACC, Adaptive Cross-Modal Calibration. Even after removing the most problematic modality-specific channels, the remaining shared channels still exhibit residual cross-modal mismatch. ACC adds two lightweight learnable branches: one addresses weight-side quantization errors that become amplified under cross-modal shifts, and the other compensates activation-side deviations, especially for text tokens. The design choice is pragmatic. Instead of introducing a heavy joint optimization scheme, the method keeps the PTQ setting intact and adds small corrective modules around the quantized path. Relative to prior transformation-based PTQ, the novelty is not just an extra calibration block; it is the sequence of first decoupling modality-specific channels, then calibrating the residual shared space.2c:T4d6,Experiments are conducted on Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Qwen2.5-VL-3B across six mainstream benchmarks, under W4A8, W4A4, W3A3, and W3A2 settings. The headline result is that W4A4 nearly preserves FP16 accuracy: the average score is 73.5 vs. 74.3 FP16 on Qwen2.5-VL-7B, and 69.6 vs. 70.0 FP16 on Qwen2.5-VL-3B. That gap is small enough to suggest the method substantially suppresses cross-modal quantization error. More importantly, the method remains effective at W3A3 and W3A2, which indicates the gain is not a mild high-bit refinement but a more robust fix for aggressive low-bit distortion.

The ablation logic appears to have three layers. First, channel-distribution analysis establishes that modality heterogeneity is concentrated in a small and misaligned subset of channels. Second, MOCD contributes the bulk of the gain, supporting the claim that explicit channel-level decoupling is more appropriate than forcing a shared transformation. Third, ACC further reduces residual error in the main channels, with stronger recovery on the text side. When reading the full results, W4A4 and W3A3 are the most informative settings because they best expose structural improvements rather than letting higher precision hide quantization mistakes.2d:T479,Multi-token Residual Prediction (MRP) leverages the empirical fact that the logit change between consecutive denoising steps is small when only a few tokens are unmasked. Define the inter-step residual δ_t = ℓ_{t-1} − ℓ_t. Since δ_t has much smaller magnitude than ℓ_t, predicting δ_t is an easier target.
At inference, one backbone forward produces ℓ_t and hidden states h_t; a lightweight MRP head predicts a correction term from h_t so that ℓ_{t-1} can be approximated as ℓ_t + MRP(h_t) without a second backbone pass. This enables decoding additional tokens “as if” the model had advanced one more denoising step.
Two modes:
- Direct decoding: use corrected logits directly for faster multi-token reveals, accepting some approximation error.
- Speculative decoding: treat MRP as a drafter and verify with the backbone on the next step; accepted tokens are intended to be lossless, shifting compute from expensive backbone passes to a cheap proposal mechanism.
Training uses a residual-focused objective supervised by true adjacent-step logits (details matter: scaling/normalization and what exact residual is predicted).2e:T405,Mix-Quant applies NVFP4 weight-and-activation quantization only during prefilling, while keeping decoding in BF16. The rationale is straightforward but well argued. Prefill processes a fixed context in parallel and is compute-bound, so low-bit matmuls can directly reduce latency. Decode is autoregressive and more sensitive because each generated token becomes future input; quantization error therefore propagates across the trajectory. The paper also argues that agentic contexts contain substantial redundancy—conversation history, tool outputs, retrieved passages, environment state—so prefill can tolerate more approximation. Compared with weight-only INT4 PTQ, this approach can accelerate compute-heavy prefill rather than only memory-bound decode. Compared with full-path low-bit quantization, it avoids error accumulation in generation. The novelty is less about inventing a new quantizer and more about decomposing inference into phases and matching hardware support, workload structure, and numerical sensitivity.2f:T782,The core method is a staged post-training pipeline. The authors build three disjoint datasets for visual perception, textual reasoning, and visual reasoning, and train them sequentially instead of merging all data into a single mixed stage. The most important stage is visual perception. Its goal is not to produce fluent captions, but to make the model reliably identify fine-grained entities, attributes, and spatial relations and align them correctly with text.

To do this, they start from fine-grained image-caption resources such as DOCCI and convert image-caption pairs into perception-focused QA data rather than directly applying caption SFT. They then compare caption-based SFT with RLVR, reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Their argument is that caption SFT imposes token-level, off-policy imitation signals that can pull the model toward a narrower or lower-quality supervision distribution than its pretraining corpus, while RL keeps learning on-policy and is better suited for “see correctly, then answer” behaviors where correctness is externally verifiable.

The second stage is textual reasoning, which strengthens pure language reasoning so that visual errors and textual reasoning errors are not entangled. The third stage is visual reasoning, where the model combines the earlier perception and reasoning skills on image-conditioned tasks. The paper also compares staged training against one-stage merged training and studies stage order. The conclusion is that order matters: perception must be solidified before visual reasoning, otherwise later reasoning is built on faulty visual representations.

A useful conceptual contribution is the framing of this setup as capability-dimension curriculum learning. Instead of ordering samples by difficulty, it orders optimization by capability type. The paper further shows that this is complementary to standard difficulty-based curricula.30:T640,The most convincing part is the initial error attribution. Using Claude-Haiku-4.5 to analyze incorrect outputs from Qwen3-VL-8B on three visual math datasets, the authors estimate that 86.9% of the errors are caused by perception failures rather than downstream reasoning. That directly motivates the staged design.

In terms of performance, adding a visual perception stage gives a 7.43-point gain over the Qwen2.5-VL-7B base model on WeMath, and raises Qwen3-VL-8B from 50.9% to 56.1%. In the staged-versus-one-stage comparison, staged Qwen3-VL-8B improves math reasoning accuracy by 1.46 points while producing 20.8% shorter reasoning traces, which suggests the gain comes from better perception rather than longer deliberation. The stage-order ablation is also important: disrupting the order drops average visual math performance of Qwen2.5-VL-7B from 42.3% to 37.7%, supporting the claim that perception is a scaffold rather than just another training component.

For training objective comparison, replacing RL with SFT in the visual perception stage causes WeMath accuracy drops of 8.1% for Qwen2.5-VL-7B and 1.6% for Qwen3-VL-8B. This supports the claim that RLVR is a better signal than caption SFT for perception learning. The final staged Qwen3-VL-8B reaches 75.9% on MathVista, 56.1% on WeMath, and 74.5% on RealWorldQA. Relative to OneThinker-8B, it improves by 1.5% on WeMath and 3.0% on RealWorldQA. The curriculum result is also notable: combining capability-based staging with difficulty-based ordering yields a 4.43% gain over merged training, outperforming either dimension alone.31:T7ef,方法核心是 StitchVM。结构上分三段：
1. frozen diffusion head：取一个预训练 diffusion 或 flow 模型的前半段/截断段，用来处理 noisy latent zt。因为这部分网络天然适配不同噪声水平的输入，所以它负责把 noisy latent 编码成中间特征。
2. pretrained reward tail：取一个已经训练好的 reward model 的后半段，使其保留强语义和偏好判断能力。这个 reward model 原本工作在像素空间或解码图像上。
3. stitching layer：在 head 与 tail 之间插入一个轻量映射层，把 diffusion feature 对齐到 reward feature 空间。

关键难点不是“接起来”，而是“接在哪一层”。作者借鉴 model stitching 文献，寻找一个 representation compatibility 较高的 stitch point。判断标准是：在某些层，diffusion head 的特征是否可以通过近似线性变换映射到 reward tail 的特征。如果可以，那么先用闭式解或线性回归拟合一个初始映射，再做短程 finetune，就有机会在不破坏 reward tail 预测能力的前提下，让整个 stitched model 直接对 noisy latent 输出 value。

这个设计相对已有方法的区别很明确：
- 不像 Tweedie，它不需要额外 denoiser 前向和 VAE decode，再把估计出的 x0 送进 reward model，因此推理链更短。
- 不像 MC，它不需要从 zt rollout 多条完整去噪轨迹，省掉大量采样成本。
- 不像已有直接 value model，它不从小规模 noisy-latent 数据从头训练一个新模型，也不只是简单复用 diffusion feature 做一个浅层头，而是显式把 foundation reward model 的后段能力迁移过来。

从全文叙述看，作者强调两个训练阶段：先做 stitch point 搜索和线性初始化，确认 head/tail 在某层可对齐；再做小成本 finetuning，闭合剩余表征差距。这样做的原因很直接：如果一开始端到端训练，容易把 reward tail 原有能力破坏掉，也会失去“低成本迁移”的意义。32:T72d,The proposed method, StitchVM, has three components.
1. A frozen diffusion head: a truncated prefix of a pretrained diffusion or flow model that naturally processes noisy latents at different timesteps.
2. A pretrained reward tail: a sliced suffix of a strong reward model that already knows how to predict prompt fidelity, aesthetics, or other downstream rewards from image-space features.
3. A stitching layer: a lightweight mapping inserted between the two so that diffusion features can be fed into the reward tail.

The main technical issue is where to stitch. The paper borrows from model stitching and looks for layers where the diffusion-head representation can be mapped to the reward-tail representation with a near-linear transform. If such a point exists, the bridge can be initialized analytically or with simple regression, and only a short finetuning phase is needed to close the remaining gap. This preserves most of the reward model’s predictive skill while making it operate directly on noisy latents.

This differs from prior alternatives in a clean way:
- Unlike Tweedie-based methods, it does not need an extra denoiser pass plus VAE decoding before reward evaluation.
- Unlike Monte Carlo value estimation, it avoids expensive denoising rollouts from each zt.
- Unlike prior learned value models, it is not trained from scratch on noisy-latent supervision at small scale, nor does it merely attach a shallow head to diffusion features. Instead, it explicitly reuses the upper layers of a pretrained reward model.

The training recipe appears to have two stages: identify a compatible stitch point and fit an initial linear bridge, then run a short finetuning stage. That choice is important. End-to-end training from the start would risk destroying the reward tail and would weaken the claim of low-cost transfer.33:T76f,From the available full-text portion, the paper evaluates StitchVM across multiple diffusion backbones, including SD 3.5 Medium and SD 3.5 Large, and positions it as useful for both training-time alignment and inference-time guidance. Even without the full result tables here, the experimental agenda is clear.

First, value estimation quality. StitchVM should be compared against Tweedie approximations, Monte Carlo estimates, and prior learned value models, especially as a function of noise level. The decisive test is whether it remains accurate in high-noise regimes where Tweedie is known to be biased.

Second, downstream alignment quality. Since the value model is meant to guide optimization, the paper should measure prompt fidelity, aesthetic preference, or related reward-aligned metrics under training-time or inference-time alignment. The practical claim is that better noisy-latent value estimates translate into better reward optimization at similar or lower compute.

Third, transfer and generalization. Because the method is sold as a reusable interface between pretrained diffusion models and pretrained reward models, the important checks are:
- whether one reward tail can be stitched to multiple diffusion backbones,
- whether changing the reward model still requires only short finetuning,
- how sensitive performance is to stitch-point selection,
- and how much of the gain comes from linear initialization versus subsequent finetuning.

When reading the full paper, the most informative ablations are likely the stitch depth, frozen versus trainable components, the effect of closed-form initialization, error curves across timesteps, and compute-versus-accuracy trade-offs against MC rollouts. For this kind of work, the key question is not just whether one metric improves, but whether reward-model capability is truly transferred into the noisy latent regime in a robust way.34:T586,Read it with one concrete question in mind: is this merely a cheaper reward proxy, or a genuinely reusable noisy-latent value model? Start with the overview figure and method section to understand the head-tail-stitch decomposition and how it differs from Tweedie, MC, and direct learned value models.

Then focus on stitch-point selection. This is the technical hinge of the paper. If the compatibility criterion is principled and the linear bridge works consistently, the method is much more convincing. Pay attention to how representation compatibility is measured and why a linear map is sufficient as an initialization.

Next, inspect two result groups: value-prediction accuracy across noise levels, and downstream alignment quality versus compute. The first tells you whether it really fixes the high-noise weakness of Tweedie. The second tells you whether it offers a practical alternative to MC.

Finally, read the ablations on backbone choice, reward-tail choice, stitch depth, freezing strategy, and finetuning budget. Those are the parts most relevant if you want to reuse the idea in other multimodal or latent-space settings.

A solid skim should take 35 to 50 minutes. If you only want the main idea, the method figure, stitch-point analysis, and main result tables are enough. If you want to adapt the approach elsewhere, spend extra time on representation alignment and training stability details.35:T526,The authors instantiate a canonical-looking audit pipeline with three ingredients. First, they LoRA-memorize 60 fictional author biographies into DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, using a reasoning format where the model restates the target biography in the <think> segment and then gives an answer. Second, they apply NPO unlearning with the forgetting loss masked to the answer span only; no forgetting gradient directly touches the biography template inside the trace. Third, they evaluate generations by splitting on </think> and performing substring matching separately in the trace and the answer. This setup structurally favors a positive bypass gap because the trace template is left largely untouched while the answer span is optimized away. The key technical move is to add fixed-weight probes. One probe swaps the model-generated trace for short non-canary prefills at inference time and checks whether answer continuation remains invariant. Another uses teacher-forced continuation scoring under the same six-token canary head, avoiding greedy decoding and parser dependence. The canary head is important: the metric is a head-conditioned continuation score, not a free-recall measure. A second model, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, is used to show that formatting drift alone can invert the parser-split metric.36:T5fc,The main results form a compact measurement story. On Qwen-7B, after LoRA memorization, out_acc is 1.00. After NPO, answer-side out_acc falls to 0.60, while trace-side thk_leak remains 0.83, giving a parser-split gap of Δ=+0.23. At first glance this looks like a thinking-channel bypass. However, in 86% of those bypass cases, the answer span is merely a verbatim copy of the question prefix with no real continuation, while the trace contains the trained biography. A fixed-weight prefill swap then changes the answer rate substantially: a short non-canary prefill lowers out_acc from 0.60 to 0.37; bio-style and meta-style prefills land at similar rates; an empty prefill lowers it to 0.20. On a second seed, the bypass gap shrinks rather than vanishes, and the prefill effect reverses direction, bringing the answer rate close to ceiling. Teacher-forced scoring tells a different story from greedy substring matching: after NPO, top-1 continuation match under the same six-token canary head stays between 0.90 and 1.00 across prefills. On Llama-8B, the parser-split metric flips to -0.92 at K=1600, while out_acc remains 1.00 and teacher-forced top-1 is at least 0.998. The cause is format drift: in at least 60% of probes, the biography template moves outside the <think> tags, leaving the parser with an empty trace. The authors also note that prefills perturb ordinary QA probes, so the intervention is not a mechanism isolator; it rules out the stronger claim that the parser-split gap alone proves hidden-channel retention.37:T419,Starting from the properties of KL divergence, the work compares the mode-seeking property of reverse KL and the mode-covering property of forward KL, pointing out that forward KL minimization is the theoretically optimal solution to avoid mode collapse. However, directly optimizing forward KL is computationally intractable, as it requires sampling from a global reward-weighted distribution over trajectories. Accordingly, DMPO (Distribution-Matching Policy Optimization) is proposed to approximate forward KL minimization at the group level: for each group of G trajectories sampled per query, a Boltzmann distribution is constructed as the target distribution based on rewards. A distribution-matching regularization term is added to the standard GRPO objective to align the policy distribution with this intra-group target distribution. This method is naturally compatible with GRPO's existing group-level advantage normalization logic, and can be implemented with only minor code modifications, requiring no additional model or data overhead.38:T46a,Evaluation benchmarks include two categories. First, MM-NP-Bench, a multimodal combinatorial optimization benchmark covering 10 NP-hard problems across 5 categories (Routing, Covering, Partitioning, Substructure, Constraints), is constructed. It includes parametric generators, rule-based verifiers, and heuristic solver baselines, and uses a dual-metric evaluation of Success Rate (SR) and Quality Ratio (QR), where QR quantifies the optimality of solutions and identifies mode collapse (high SR with low QR indicates mode collapse). Second, mathematical reasoning and cross-domain transfer tasks are used for further validation. Experiments compare 5 strong baselines including GRPO: on NP-Bench, DMPO achieves a Quality Ratio of 43.9%, a 9% relative improvement over GRPO's 40.1%; on MM-NP-Bench, DMPO achieves 43.1% QR, a 12% relative improvement over GRPO's 38.4%; mathematical reasoning accuracy improves by 2.0%, and out-of-domain task accuracy improves by 2.3%. Ablation studies verify the contribution of the distribution-matching regularization term and the effectiveness of group-level target distribution construction.39:T6cb,ReCrit builds a four-quadrant transition reward over two generations. For each question, the policy first produces an initial solution y0. It then receives critic feedback c and generates a critic-stage solution y1 conditioned on the question, the initial answer, and the feedback. A judge labels both y0 and y1 as correct or incorrect, and the pair is mapped into one of four transition types: Correction (wrong to right), Sycophancy (right to wrong), Robustness (right to right), and Boundary (wrong to wrong). Training assigns calibrated rewards to these quadrants: strong positive reward for Correction, strong penalty for Sycophancy, weak positive reward for Robustness, and weak penalty for Boundary.

The design choice is deliberate. If one only rewards final correctness, the model can improve by becoming more willing to change its answer, which may increase both useful corrections and harmful capitulations. The quadrant reward separates these behaviors explicitly. The paper also samples different critic styles, including opposing, neutral, and supportive feedback, so the policy must learn conditional revision rather than unconditional deference. Optimization uses group-normalized advantages, suggesting a GRPO-style relative update rather than plain scalar-reward PPO. On the systems side, the authors introduce dynamic asynchronous rollout with tail-adaptive completion: samples that finish the initial stage can move to the critic stage immediately, instead of waiting for the slowest trajectories in the batch. Compared with SFT, the key difference is explicit cross-turn credit assignment. Compared with final-answer RL, the difference is that the objective is defined over transition structure, not just the endpoint.3a:T509,The evaluation uses three closed-form scientific reasoning benchmarks: ChemBench, TRQA, and EarthSE, covering chemistry, biology, and earth science. Models are Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3.5-9B. The main metric is critic accuracy, meaning accuracy after receiving critic feedback. On 4B models, average critic accuracy rises from 38.15 to 51.49, a gain of 13.34 points. On 9B models, it rises from 45.40 to 55.59, a gain of 10.19 points. The introduction also gives a motivating failure case: on biology-related reasoning, Qwen3-max turns 6.98% of initially correct answers into wrong ones after criticism, and overall accuracy drops from 51.16 to 48.84.

The ablations are the most informative part. Final-answer reward alone yields little benefit, which supports the paper’s claim that endpoint-only supervision is too coarse. Transition-aware rewards improve net gain more substantially. Adding calibrated quadrant weights, critic-stage weighting, and finalization further improves results. Another useful observation is that SFT tends to increase both Correction and Sycophancy: the model becomes more revision-prone, but not necessarily better at deciding when revision is warranted. ReCrit improves the balance by making the reward signal denser and more separable across transition types.3b:T7a5,The method, Conditional Entropy Shaping (CES), is built on top of DAPO. The starting observation is that a small number of high-entropy tokens in a reasoning trace often act as forking points, where the trajectory could branch in different directions. Prior work often rewards such tokens uniformly to preserve exploration, but that also encourages already-correct trajectories to remain diffuse and verbose. CES changes this by making the shaping policy both conditional and bidirectional.

During training, the model first receives a response-level correctness signal. Token-level entropy is then used to reshape the advantage:
1. For correct trajectories, high-entropy tokens are penalized. The intuition is that if a path already leads to the right answer, the model should become more confident and direct at those branching points rather than keep exploring unnecessarily.
2. For incorrect trajectories, high-entropy tokens are rewarded. The intuition is that these branching points are exactly where more exploration is useful, since the current path failed.

This differs sharply from a plain length penalty. Length penalties only care about output size and cannot distinguish necessary deliberation from wasteful expansion. CES instead tries to intervene at the uncertain decision points. It also differs from uniform entropy encouragement, which assumes exploration is always beneficial. CES says exploration should depend on whether the current reasoning path is already successful.

Algorithmically, CES does not replace DAPO. It augments DAPO’s token-level advantage shaping with a correctness-by-entropy conditional term. DAPO itself is an engineering refinement of GRPO for stable long-CoT RL; CES adds a mechanism to control the exploration-versus-conciseness trade-off inside that framework. The paper emphasizes that this is token-level, conditional, and bidirectional, rather than a query-level hard router or a post-hoc pruning method.3c:T634,The evaluation covers 12 math benchmarks. The main claim is that CES improves the average accuracy-efficiency trade-off over DAPO: it maintains or improves accuracy while reducing unnecessary reasoning length. The provided text does not include the full result tables, but the framing is clear that the gain is not merely shorter outputs; it is a better joint operating point on correctness and token cost.

The paper also reports two useful robustness checks. First, it evaluates a smaller 1.5B backbone, suggesting the method is not tied only to larger reasoning models. Second, it includes out-of-domain benchmarks, indicating that CES is learning a more general adaptive reasoning behavior rather than overfitting to a narrow training distribution.

The analysis section appears to be important. The authors argue that CES learns an on-demand reasoning policy: shorter traces on easy problems, more exploration on hard problems or on failing trajectories. That is a stronger claim than simply reducing average length. If the main tables support the introduction’s summary, the most informative plots are likely those showing the joint movement of accuracy and token usage, plus length distributions stratified by problem difficulty.

Because the supplied full-text excerpt cuts off before the detailed results, exact gains, token reductions, training steps, and sampling-group settings cannot be quoted reliably here. What can be stated with confidence is that DAPO is the baseline, the evaluation spans 12 math benchmarks, and the paper includes 1.5B-scale and out-of-domain tests.3d:T4ca,For a skim, start with the introduction’s framing of the two opposing lines of work: entropy-driven exploration helps accuracy but lengthens CoT, while efficiency-oriented RL shortens outputs but can hurt hard problems. Once that tension is clear, CES’s conditional design becomes much easier to parse.

In the method section, focus on the advantage-shaping formula and the selection of high-entropy tokens. Three implementation details matter most: how correct versus incorrect trajectories are determined, how high-entropy tokens are selected, and how the shaping term is combined with DAPO’s original advantage. If these choices are unstable, training could easily collapse into uniformly short or uniformly verbose behavior.

In the experiments, prioritize the main results table, any plots of response length distributions, difficulty-bucket analyses, and the 1.5B plus out-of-domain results. A useful skim should take about 20 to 30 minutes. For deeper reading, probe two questions: whether CES truly shortens easy cases while preserving depth on hard ones, rather than just averaging to a compromise; and whether token entropy is a reliable proxy for reasoning uncertainty rather than merely stylistic hesitation.3e:T4ab,CopT first samples a draft answer. It then compares the model’s support for the same generated tokens under two input views: the normal discrete-token prefix and a continuous prefix built from cached next-token distributions. The contrast gives a sequence-level reverse-KL estimator for draft reliability. If the draft passes the criterion, it is accepted early. Otherwise, the model enters an on-policy thinking phase. During that phase, CopT does not expose the draft answer at every step. It periodically recomputes a related KL score within thinking chunks and uses it to control whether the draft remains visible. This design differs from latent-CoT methods that directly generate through continuous hidden states. CopT uses continuous states only as uncertainty-preserving probes, reducing the risk of off-distribution latent inputs, loss of diversity, and noisy drift. The paper also gives a latent-state interpretation: under a local mixture-prefix view and a mixture-linear assumption, the estimator corresponds to mutual information between the unresolved reasoning state and the emitted answer token, so the score reflects answer-relevant uncertainty rather than raw latent entropy.3f:T67c,The work has two components. First is the MixRea benchmark. Each example contains a story context with implicit information, a hypothetical question with explicit information, and four candidate event sets. Each candidate consists of two events, and the correct choice must satisfy both the explicit query and the latent contextual cues. The benchmark is built on Possible Stories, which is a good substrate because multiple questions share the same context and answer options, making it easier to recombine explicit and implicit constraints in a controlled way. The authors filter conflicting question pairs, then use GPT-4o for rewriting, pairing, validation, and quality control, producing 2,246 multiple-choice questions across 9 reasoning types with varied explicit/implicit information distributions.

Second is PRCP, Potential Relation Completion Prompting. Instead of asking the model to simply reason longer, PRCP explicitly asks it to surface possible latent relations before making the final choice. The paper’s error analysis suggests that many failures happen early: the model never completes the hidden causal relation needed to connect the context to the task. PRCP is designed to externalize that step. Compared with standard CoT, it is more targeted at relation elicitation; compared with one-shot prompting, it reduces the tendency to answer by shallow pattern matching. The prompt itself is not the main contribution. Its real role is causal diagnosis: if performance improves when latent relation completion is forced, then the bottleneck is likely in cue retrieval and multi-source integration rather than only in final answer selection.40:T58f,The benchmark is evaluated on 21 models from 7 families, including Gemini 2.5 Pro/Flash, DeepSeek-R1/V3, GPT-4o/4o-mini, Claude 3.7, Qwen variants, LLaMA 3.x, and Gemma 2/3. The headline result is that performance is low across the board. Even the best model, Gemini 2.5 Pro, reaches only 42.8% consistency, which indicates that explicit-implicit integration remains unreliable even for strong reasoning-oriented models. Reasoning models generally outperform non-reasoning ones, but not by enough to remove the failure mode, suggesting that longer or more polished reasoning traces do not automatically translate into better use of implicit cues.

PRCP outperforms both CoT and one-shot prompting more consistently. The abstract does not expose the full gain table, but the full text presents the improvement as broad rather than model-specific. The authors also run ablations on three additional tasks involving explicit and implicit information, and similar failures persist there, which argues against MixRea being a narrow dataset artifact. The error analysis is the most informative part: models often understand the explicit question and can restate the context, yet fail to connect latent causal cues to the task objective. Without that analysis, one might misread the problem as weak commonsense knowledge; the paper makes a more precise case that the issue lies in relation completion and multi-source integration.41:T4be,They introduce CAPE (Capability Coupling Analysis of Phase Emergence). The key metric is *local coupling* within a family: γ12(N)=ΔTruthfulQA/ΔHellaSwag between adjacent sizes, i.e., a derivative of one capability with respect to another rather than with respect to N. They distinguish this from *population coupling* r(B1,B2), a Pearson correlation across a panel of models.
Empirically, γ12 is negative at small scale and crosses zero at a recipe-dependent critical size Nc, then grows roughly linearly with log10(N/Nc): γ12(N,D)=γ0(D)·log10(N/Nc(D)), with both slope and Nc depending on the training recipe D.
They argue Nc is independently shiftable by (i) data curation (Qwen generations), (ii) architecture/distillation/post-training (Gemma-4 at 4B showing “large-model-like” coupling), and (iii) width at fixed parameter count (width-normalized coupling being more consistently positive).
For mechanism, they report little evidence of “competing attention heads” in most models and suggest the bottleneck is closer to the output projection/readout than internal attention competition. They also fit a sparse-regression ODE to coupling dynamics and report cross-prediction on held-out Llama-2.42:T40e,The study aggregates public benchmark results over 63 base models from 16 families. Reported headline numbers include:
- Pythia (70M–12B, 8 models): reasoning vs truthfulness population correlation r=-0.989 (p<1e-5).
- Larger models across families (e.g., Llama/Falcon/OPT above ~7B): correlation becomes strongly positive (stated as r>+0.78), and a “frontier” panel of 34 models from 10 labs yields r=+0.72.
- Estimated critical scale Nc ≈ 3.5B parameters with bootstrap 95% CI [2.9B, 13.4B], emphasizing strong family/recipe dependence.
- Recipe shift examples: Qwen coupling at matched scale improves from 0.025 to 0.830 across generations; Gemma-4 at 4B reports coupling 0.871, described as typical of standard-trained 13B+; OLMo sits near γ12=0.000 at the predicted transition scale.
- Sparse ODE cross-predicts held-out Llama-2 with 5.6% error (as defined by the authors).
Overall, the evidence is correlational/observational, but the authors’ claim is that coupling is strong enough to be stable despite benchmark noise.43:T5c0,The setup is simple. Given an image, a question, and a partial reasoning trace, a base MLLM proposes a candidate next reasoning step. Three frozen verifier agents then score that step from different perspectives: visual grounding, logical coherence, and contextual relevance. Instead of averaging these scores, the paper models each verifier as a player in a coordination game. Each player wants to stay close to the others, but also wants to remain faithful to its own initial judgment. This trade-off is encoded with a quadratic utility: one term penalizes deviation from the mean opinion of the other agents, and another penalizes deviation from the agent’s own raw score, with a coefficient lambda controlling how stubborn the agent is. The Nash equilibrium of this game yields adjusted scores that reflect both confidence and residual disagreement, and the system uses those equilibrium scores to accept or reject the candidate step.

The important part is not the game-theory label by itself. The real contribution is to make disagreement structurally visible. A step that looks visually plausible but logically incoherent should not receive the same treatment as a step that all judges independently support. The paper also emphasizes that the equilibrium has a closed-form solution, so the extra computation is negligible relative to calling the verifier models. The whole method is plug-in: no fine-tuning, no labeled data, and no modification to the base MLLM.44:T792,The method is built around the Information Bottleneck (IB) principle. A reasoning trajectory X is the input, a compressed representation Z corresponds to the trajectory with stepwise confidence weights, and final correctness Y is the target. The goal is to retain the parts of the trajectory that are most informative about correctness while discarding superficial variation and redundancy.

The first instantiation is a non-parametric method, NIBS. For a given problem, it aggregates multiple correct solution traces and extracts consensus steps or logical invariants. Each step in a candidate trajectory is then scored by how well it aligns with this consensus structure. Steps that match shared reasoning milestones receive high confidence; steps absent from or deviating from the consensus receive low confidence. The key idea is functional alignment rather than literal overlap. In IB terms, the consensus set approximates the relevant information, and the selected sparse subset approximates the compressed representation.

The second instantiation is GIBS, a Graph-IB variant. The authors argue that a pure sequence view is brittle when correct solutions reorder steps or use more graph-like reasoning structures. They therefore represent trajectories as graphs and learn a differentiable mask to select a subgraph most predictive of correctness. Confidence is then derived by aligning this selected subgraph with the correctness signal. This makes the method more tolerant to structural variability and closer to extracting a logical backbone than scoring a linear chain.

A practical extension is self-correction. Instead of telling the model only that the final answer is unreliable, SCA highlights low-confidence steps and uses them as repair targets. That explains why step-level confidence can outperform answer-level feedback: the model gets a localized signal about where to revise rather than a single global failure flag.45:T6ff,The empirical section has four main parts. First, step-level diagnostic quality. The paper compares SCA against answer-level confidence estimation and other black-box confidence baselines, and shows that both IB-based variants identify erroneous steps more accurately, especially when correct traces vary substantially in wording or order. The graph-based version is reported to be more robust under stronger structural variation.

Second, self-correction. This is the most consequential result in the paper. Using step-level confidence to guide correction improves correction success rate by up to 13.5% over answer-level feedback. That result matters because it shows the signal is not only more interpretable, but also operationally useful.

Third, ablations. The authors study the role of consensus extraction, the IB formulation, and graph structure. Removing consensus modeling makes the method more likely to confuse surface similarity with valid logic. Removing graph structure hurts robustness to step permutations and alternative reasoning layouts. They also analyze label availability and the number of correct traces needed to form a stable consensus, showing that dense step annotations are unnecessary but some pool of correct trajectories is still important.

Fourth, robustness and generalization. The paper evaluates different reasoning formats, domain shift, and limited-label settings. The main takeaway is that SCA transfers beyond a single CoT format, while GIBS is stronger when reasoning structure varies more substantially. In the provided text, the only explicit hard number is the 13.5% gain in correction success over answer-level feedback. Exact model sizes, dataset sizes, and full benchmark scores would need to be read from the result tables in the full paper.46:T4f5,For a skim, focus on three things. First, read the task definition in the introduction and problem statement carefully. The paper is not doing step correctness classification in the usual supervised sense, and it is not asking an LLM judge to rate each step. It uses final correctness to anchor stepwise confidence. That distinction is important.

Second, compare NIBS and GIBS in the method section. The main question is how the paper turns the idea of consensus logical structure into something computable. If time is limited, understand the non-parametric version first, since it captures the core intuition most directly. Then look at the graph version to see what kinds of structural variability it is meant to handle.

Third, read the self-correction results and the robustness section. The former answers whether the signal is useful; the latter answers whether it only works in a narrow format. A 20 to 30 minute pass should cover the introduction, method overview figure, main result table, self-correction table, and ablations. If reading more deeply, two questions are worth probing: whether consensus-based scoring unfairly penalizes rare but valid solution paths, and whether this signal could be turned into a process reward or a verifier-training target.47:T98b,作者研究的是 Kulumba 等人训练的一组对比式 authorship attribution 模型。它们共享同一个预训练 encoder、同样的数据和 InfoNCE 训练目标，差别只在 scoring 机制：

1. Mean pooling：先把 passage 的 token 表示取平均，再做 cosine similarity。它要求 encoder 在单个向量里压缩整段文本的信息。
2. Late interaction, LI：保留 token-level 表示，用 MaxSim 之类的 token-to-token 匹配来打分。它不要求所有信息都提前压进一个全局向量。
3. Patch pooling：介于两者之间，把相邻 token 组成 patch 后先局部平均，再做 late interaction。

分析工具主要有三类。

第一类是线性 probe，用来测“风格特征是否存在于各层表示中”。作者探测的特征包括词长、标点密度、功能词频率、大小写等。结果显示，这些特征在所有模型、几乎所有层、甚至一个未针对该任务微调的 control encoder 中都能被线性读出。也就是说，fine-tuning 并没有创造这些特征。

第二类是 causal intervention，具体是 residual stream patching。做法是：给定 anchor、positive、negative 三元组，用 clean positive 和 corrupt negative 分别前向；然后在某一层把 negative 的隐藏状态替换成 positive 的对应状态，再看最终 same-author 评分恢复多少。这个实验测的是“某层表示对正确作者判断的因果贡献”。结果表明，mean pooling 模型在较早到中间层就已经把 authorship signal 整合到足以影响最终判断；LI 模型则把这种整合推迟到更后层，因为它可以依赖 token-level 匹配，不必过早压缩。

第三类是从梯度结构解释上述差异。mean pooling 的梯度会把所有 token 通过平均操作耦合起来，迫使中早层就形成可全局汇聚的作者风格表示；LI 的梯度更局部，允许不同 token 保留细粒度风格线索，到后层或 scorer 端再完成利用。patch pooling 则处在中间，形成一个连续谱，而不是二元对立。

论文还结合 alignment–uniformity 分析训练动态。作者观察到，不同 scorer 不只是最终 readout 不同，而是沿着不同的优化路径塑造 encoder：mean pooling 更早追求全局对齐，LI 则允许表示空间在更长阶段内保留局部结构。这解释了为什么两者即便共享 backbone，最终有效深度和可利用信息也不同。48:Ta44,The study analyzes a family of contrastive authorship-attribution models from prior work. They share the same pretrained encoder, the same training data, and the same InfoNCE objective. The only difference is the scoring mechanism:

1. Mean pooling: token representations are averaged into a single passage vector, then compared with cosine similarity. This forces the encoder to compress passage-level information into one vector.
2. Late interaction (LI): token-level representations are preserved and compared with a MaxSim-style token-to-token scorer. This avoids early global compression.
3. Patch pooling: an intermediate design where contiguous token patches are mean-pooled first, then scored with late interaction.

The analysis uses three main tools.

First, linear probes test whether stylistic features are present in hidden states across layers. The probed features include word length, punctuation density, function-word frequency, capitalization, and related style cues. The result is that these features are linearly recoverable from nearly all layers in all models, including an off-the-shelf control encoder. Fine-tuning does not create them.

Second, the paper uses causal intervention via residual stream patching. Given an anchor, a same-author positive, and a different-author negative, the model runs a clean forward pass on the positive and a corrupt pass on the negative. At a chosen layer, the negative hidden state is replaced with the positive hidden state, and the final score recovery is measured. This identifies where in the encoder the relevant authorship signal becomes causally important. Mean-pooling models consolidate that signal by early-to-mid layers, whereas LI models defer consolidation to later layers because token-level matching remains available downstream.

Third, the paper explains the difference through gradient structure. Mean pooling couples all token representations through the averaging operation, so the encoder is pressured to form globally aggregatable authorship features relatively early. LI produces more local gradients and allows fine-grained stylistic cues to remain distributed across tokens until later layers or the scorer itself. Patch pooling behaves as an intermediate case, which supports the gradient-based explanation.

The paper also studies training dynamics through alignment and uniformity. The claim is that different scorers do not merely read out the same encoder differently at the end; they drive the encoder along different optimization trajectories. Mean pooling pushes earlier global alignment, while LI preserves local structure for longer.49:T723,The key empirical findings, based on the provided full-text excerpt, are as follows.

1. Performance gap: with the same backbone, data, and loss, changing only the scorer can produce an approximately four-fold difference in authorship-attribution performance. This is the motivating puzzle.

2. Feature availability: linear probes show that stylistic features such as word length, punctuation, and function-word usage are readable from all models at nearly all layers, and even from an off-the-shelf control encoder. So the gap cannot be explained simply by one scorer learning better style representations.

3. Causal localization across layers: residual stream patching shows that in mean-pooling models, authorship signal becomes causally necessary by early or middle layers, whereas in LI models the critical contribution is shifted later. This is more informative than probing because it distinguishes readable information from actually used information.

4. Patch pooling as an intermediate case: bigram patch pooling (n=2) behaves between mean pooling and LI, which strengthens the claim that the effect is driven by scorer-induced gradient structure rather than an arbitrary architectural quirk.

5. Training dynamics: alignment/uniformity analysis indicates that the scorers induce different optimization trajectories during fine-tuning. The excerpt does not expose all numerical details, but the paper’s argument is that the main difference lies in when the model starts compressing authorship signal and when the representation space loses local detail.

The experimental strength of the paper is the coherence of the evidence chain: probes show the features are already there, patching shows where they become causally used, gradient analysis explains why, and training dynamics shows how the divergence develops.4a:T550,This is a good skim for researchers interested in mechanisms rather than the task itself.
